A CronJob `nightly` (every minute, 100m) and Deployments `web`, `old`, `zero` (2 pods each) approved at 200m. **Unsent reports**: the Hub refusing reports, `nightly`'s `applied` stayed in `state.json`, survived a deleted Actuator pod, and reached the Hub once it took reports, `state.json` then empty. **The Kill switch**: on, then the Hub stopped and the Actuator's pod deleted: the new Actuator, the Hub unreachable, left the next run's pod at its template's 100m (the runs before had 200m); the Hub back with it off, "the Kill switch is off". **A lone Job** `nightly-123` started at 100m beside runs at 200m. **Scaled to zero** mid-rollout, `zero` ended `applied` "1 pods resized in place, then none left to resize", and its pods came back at 200m from the webhook. **A refused approval** (run 2 at 500m under a LimitRange's 300m): `skipped` "...; run 1's requests stay held", the next run still at 200m. **A Revert, then the same run**: listed as approved before the Revert it did nothing (the next run at 100m), marked `again` beside the Revert still listed it waited. **A catch-up after an old restart** (the Actuator stopped, `web`'s `pods` and `watch_until` taken out of its ConfigMap as if its hour were over, one pod's container restarted, the other pod replaced from its template): the old pod kept in the hold as `{restarts: 1}`, no Rollback two minutes on. **The API server frozen** (`docker pause` of the control plane) with a Revert of `web` asked: each call failed once after 15 s (`0: MaxRetryError`, a POST's `0: ReadTimeoutError`), said in its log, the process never restarted; the Revert reported "0 pods back ...; 1 refused, first listing its pods: 0: ...; tried again each minute"; unfrozen, the next minute put both pods back at 100m/64Mi ("no pod left to put back"). Found, and fixed before the release: (1) the Kubernetes client's own default retried a timed-out read three times, so against the frozen API server one call held the minute four timeouts long and the Actuator made no pull for 3 min 48 s (the Kill switch unheard): its clients now try once (`single`), 15 s a call (ponytail: a minute of some twenty calls still took 3 min 45 s frozen; stop at the first if it matters); (2) the `refused` mark of 12.9a skipped every run up to the refused one, so after a refused run 2, run 1 approved again after its Revert was never applied: it now skips that run only. Both re-checked on the rebuilt image (the re-approval applied; the freeze as above). 5 more mutations caught. Not exercised: a rollout waiting out a failure at its next pod (`stalled`): a whole frozen API server fails the canary's own read first, which already waited. kind's own artefact: its DNS runs on the frozen node, so the Hub was reached by address for the freeze. Removed after: the cluster, the stand-in Hub, the image.
- 12.9c built 2026-11-11 (the audit's Hub-side findings: what a compromised Actuator's token could do with its reports, all within its own Contract and cluster). (1) `/actuator/reports` refuses, whole (422, naming the field, never the value), a report with a number that isn't one or is negative (`actuator.unreal`: `cpu`, `memory`, `at`): a NaN stored as a container's last Actuation broke the Automation page for 90 days. Checked in the route, not by the shared model (the Actuator builds an Actuation after it has resized and reads its stored ones back), and nothing an honest Actuator sends is refused, since a refused report is dropped there; found while building: FastAPI's own 422 echoes the value, and a NaN can't be said in JSON, so that answer was a 500 (which an Actuator sends again forever): the Hub's 422s now say where and why, never the value, on every route. (2) An Actuation is read as dated `least(at, received_at)` (its listing and order, the 7 days of Rollbacks, its 90 days): `at`, the Actuator's clock, stays the key that tells a report from one sent again. Before, a report dated years ahead stayed a container's last Actuation, kept its Workload's Automation ended and was never pruned. (3) Rollbacks are told from what is stored (migration 0030 `told`, true for the rows there before), after the Actuator is answered and again from each scheduler tick (30 s), beside the tick and never in it, one teller at a time: post, then mark, so a message Slack didn't take is told later (for 7 days; one more than 10 minutes late says when it was reported), not lost as before. A Contract whose post Slack didn't take rests 5 minutes, then twice as long each time, an hour at most, and one Contract's failure never stops another's. A pass reads no more rows than it may post (a count by cluster, then that cluster's first few; migration 0030's partial index on the untold). The line that a Rollback ended its Workload's Automation is part of that Rollback's message (its third in 7 days, counted from the stored rows), so a retry says it too. (4) At most 20 Rollbacks of one cluster are posted in an hour; the rest are counted in one line ("N more Rollbacks reported by <cluster>'s Actuator are not posted here ...: see the Automation page"), itself once an hour, those after it waiting for the next: before, one report of 1000 forged Rollbacks was 1000 posts inside its request. Another Contract's or cluster's posts are never spent (tested with two Contracts, each in its own channel). (5) Every string an Actuator sent (its Workload's and container's names, its reason, which can carry an API server's or a webhook's message) goes through `slack.clean`: `<!channel>`, `<@U…>` and `<url|text>` arrive as text. Found in review of the first cut: the teller ran inside the scheduler's tick, so Slack down held every Contract's Checks and deploy verification behind it, and a channel Slack refuses for good was asked every 30 seconds for a week; and a waiting flood was read whole each tick. 61 mutations, all caught, after one showed the number rule could be one condition. Decided while building (recommended defaults): 20 posts a cluster an hour, the 7 days, the "reported" suffix after 10 minutes, the ended line inside its Rollback's message, any negative number refused. Not built, offered: a cap on how many Actuations a cluster may store in an hour (a compromised Actuator can still fill its 90 days), `kind` checked against the kinds it resizes, and the reviewer's note that any later run reading a cluster supersedes an approval where the glossary says a later Sizing of that container. Not checked live: forged Rollbacks on the Mac Hub would post to a real channel; the route, the scheduler tick and Slack's failures are tested through the app.
- 12.10 grilled 2026-11-12 (found while grilling Phase 16, built before Phase 13): a pod that is NotReady because its node is gone is never a Sizing's fault. Today a resized pod NotReady for 2 minutes is a Rollback, and three end Automation: true of any node failure, frequent with spot. The tell is on the pod, with the permissions the Actuator has: while a node is NotReady the node-lifecycle controller sets only the pod's `Ready` condition to False and leaves `ContainersReady` as the kubelet last wrote it (Kubernetes v1.34 source, `MarkPodsNotReady`), where a failing readiness probe shows both; a pod removed for it carries a `DisruptionTarget` condition (`DeletionByTaintManager`, or `DeletionByPodGC` once the Node is deleted), which also covers a Failed pod in a CronJob's watched first run. How a pod reads after a graceful node shutdown (reason `Terminated`, "Pod was terminated in response to imminent node shutdown") is to confirm on kind.
- 12.10 built 2026-11-12: `lost()` tells a pod whose node is gone from the pod alone (`Ready` False with `ContainersReady` and every readiness gate True; a `DisruptionTarget` of `DeletionByTaintManager` or `DeletionByPodGC`; reason `Terminated` with the kubelet's shutdown message). Such a pod's NotReady is no Rollback, nor is a CronJob's watched run's pod that Failed that way (its OOM kill still is, and a restart from before its node went); an evicted pod's replacement lost with its node holds the 10-minute verdict. It is never given a Sizing's requests: not chosen as a canary, a rollout whose pod is lost goes on with the others and ends without it (every pod lost: as scaled to zero; a canary lost inside its ten minutes was never watched, so the next pod resized is the canary), a catch-up leaves it until its node is back, and an approval that only lost pods are missing is tried again each minute for ten (`passing`). Putting requests back (a Rollback, a Revert) still reaches it, so one whose node returns is not left with requests nobody holds (tested). Left as before, a decision not yet made: a CronJob run's pod that Failed because it was evicted to drain a node (`EvictionByEvictionAPI`, how Karpenter and Cluster Autoscaler empty one) or ended by a node under pressure (`TerminationByKubelet` without the shutdown message) still rolls its Sizing back, since a lowered request can bring the second on; a Deployment's pod removed either way is no longer listed, so it never counted. Also as it was then: a node that restarts and comes back restarts its containers, which read as a restart (seen in the live check; fixed in 12.11). No isolation test: nothing here stores, reads or sends Contract data. Mutation-checked (24 of 24 killed; a 25th showed a condition that could never differ, removed).
- 12.10 checked live 2026-11-12 on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, three workers, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac (no model calls, no cloud): Deployments `web` and `api` (3 pods, one a worker) and a CronJob `nightly` (a 15-minute run), all approved at 200m. **A worker stopped** with `web`'s canary in its ten minutes, `nightly`'s watched first run and an `api` pod not yet resized on it: its pods read `Ready` False with `ContainersReady` True and the containers ready, each with a `NodeNotReady` Event, as the source said; within the minute another `web` pod was resized and made the canary, its own ten minutes ahead; no Rollback in the 3 minutes 22 seconds watched (the canary's NotReady was one after 2 minutes before). **Five minutes on** the taint manager marked the three `DisruptionTarget: DeletionByTaintManager` and their replacements started, `nightly`'s with the held 200m from the webhook. **The Node deleted**: the three turned `Failed` with `DeletionByPodGC` ("PodGC: node no longer exists"), `nightly`'s watched run's among them: no Rollback, the hold kept; that run then ended Complete (1 succeeded, 1 failed). Both rollouts ended `3 of 3 pods resized in place`, the replacements included. **A second worker stopped** with two pods the Actuator had resized, in their watch hour: NotReady 3 minutes 30 seconds, no Rollback (the bug this slice fixes). **That worker started again** before its pods were removed, which found the case left as before to be real: its containers came back restarted (count 1, last end reason `Unknown`, exit code 255), which read as "app restarted" and rolled both Deployments back, each with its 24-hour pause. Telling a node's restart by that last end (a crashed app's reads `Error` or `OOMKilled`) is 12.11. Not produced on kind: a graceful node shutdown. Seen besides: once a CronJob's first run ends, `first_run` leaves the Actuator's memory but stays in its ConfigMap until its next save (a restart reads it, finds the run over and drops it again). The cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- 12.11 decided and built 2026-11-12 (the Operator's yes to 12.10's live finding): a container its node's own restart started again is no Rollback. `rebooted()` reads the container's last end: reason `Unknown` with exit code 255, as kind's containerd reported it once the worker was back, both needed (a crash can end with 255, and reads `Error`; an OOM kill reads `OOMKilled`). Such a restart is not counted while that is its last end; a crash or an OOM kill after it still is, and one with no last end shown is counted as before. Its NotReady is counted from when its containers stopped being ready, not from when the node went (`Ready` keeps that older time once the node is back, so an app slow to be ready again would have met the 2 minutes at once; found in review, the live check's pods were ready again in 2 seconds). Not covered: a CronJob run's pod that Failed in a node's restart (how it reads was not seen), and other runtimes' wording (CRI-O's was not looked up). No isolation test: nothing here stores, reads or sends Contract data. Mutation-checked (8 of 8 killed; a ninth could not change behaviour).
- 12.11 checked live 2026-11-12 on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, two workers, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac (no model calls, no cloud): Deployment `slow` (a pod a worker, a readiness probe that passes 60 seconds after each start), approved at 200m and applied on both. **A worker stopped for 3 minutes 56 seconds, then started**, its pod in its watch hour: down, the pod read `Ready` False with `ContainersReady` True (12.10's shape); back, its container had restarted (count 1, last end `Unknown`, exit code 255) and it was not ready for 64 seconds, `Ready` False still dated from when the node went (3 minutes before) and `ContainersReady` False from its return: the two times this slice tells apart. The Actuator's minute fell inside those 64 seconds: no Rollback, the hold still applied at 200m (the restart and the 2 minutes of NotReady were each one before). **Then the app crashed** in that pod (exit code 1, last end `Error`, its second restart): rolled back 18 seconds later, "app restarted: 2 pods back from cpu 200m to their template's cpu 100m". The cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- *Done when:* on kind 1.33+, an approved policy resizes a Deployment in place without restarts, a PDB blocks an eviction, a bad Sizing is rolled back, and pods still start with the Actuator scaled to zero.

### Phase 13: Reflexes (6 slices, grilled 2026-11-12)

- 13.1 OOM memory step-up, capped and expiring. 13.2 Node-level vs container-limit OOM attribution. 13.3 Burst CPU headroom. 13.4 Startup CPU boost. A Triage Report links any Reflex it triggered.
- Grilled 2026-11-12 (three rounds; all recommended but burst CPU, which the Operator chose to build on the Hub's side rather than defer):
  - **Every Reflex.** Only for Workloads under Automation (`automate: true`). The OOM step-up and burst CPU are switched on per Contract (`actuator: reflexes: {oom: {max: 2.0, expires: 7d}, burst: {max: 2.0, expires: 24h}}`, absent: off; refused without a cluster that runs an Actuator); the startup boost by its factor on a Sizing rule (`startup_boost: 2`, 1.5 to 4, refused without `automate`). Never under the Kill switch, on a paused Workload or for one under a VPA in any mode but `Off`; never an eviction. Step-ups and bursts together: at most 10 a cluster an hour, the rest said once and not done. ADR 0005: Reflexes may raise limits, Sizings never do (the first time the Actuator changes a limit; `pods/resize` already allows it).
  - **OOM step-up (13.1).** The memory limit and request together, by the same factor so the QoS class stays, to the larger of x1.2 and +100 MiB (VPA's numbers); every pod of the Workload at once and in place (a pod whose node has no room keeps its values), new pods through the webhook, a CronJob's next runs; every kind the Actuator handles. One step a container every 10 minutes, never past `max` times its template's limit, a LimitRange or a ResourceQuota (skipped, said), none for a container whose memory an HPA scales on, none while the Hub is unreachable (a step-up is a new change, and the Kill switch can't be heard then). The Hub lists the cluster's automated containers so the Actuator also watches those it holds nothing for; the decision is the Actuator's, within a minute. At its cap and still OOM-killed: no further step, one Slack line a day ("still OOM-killed at 2x its limit: raise it in its manifest or look for a leak"), no Rollback, Automation goes on.
  - **Attribution (13.2).** An OOM kill with a kubelet `SystemOOM` Event on its node around then is node-level; any other of a container with a memory limit is container-limit (the pod reads the same in both: `OOMKilled`, 137). One more read: Events in `default`, where Node Events are; to confirm on kind, and without it every OOM kill of a container with a limit counts as container-limit. Container-limit: a step-up and no Rollback (a Sizing never changes a limit, so it didn't cause it, and it must not count toward the three that end Automation). Node-level: a Rollback if the Actuator had lowered that container's memory request, else only said. With the Reflex off, as today.
  - **Expiry.** After `expires`, unless the Hub renews it: it renews while the container's measured memory peak is above its template's limit, so a step-up ends when the need is gone and never brings a kill on purpose. Then new pods start from the template, and running ones are lowered in place where Kubernetes allows (1.33 refuses to lower a memory limit in place; 1.34 on tries, and leaves it in progress while usage is above). Its memory values win over a held Sizing's while it lasts; at expiry the container is back at the Sizing's request, or its template's. A Revert drops it with the Sizing; a person changing the template's memory gets the container back.
  - **Startup CPU boost (13.4).** The CPU limit only, times the rule's factor, at the pod's creation (the webhook), back in place when the pod is Ready or 5 minutes after it started, whichever is first; no boost on a container's restart. Not for a Guaranteed pod (its limit can't leave its request), a container without a CPU limit (it needs none) or a CronJob. Requests untouched, so no pod is made unschedulable and no HPA or Cluster Autoscaler is misled (the pitfalls Google's kube-startup-cpu-boost documents come from raising requests). It goes on while the Hub is unreachable, as held requests do. Said in the guide: a JVM sizes its thread pools from the boosted CPU count and keeps them.
  - **Burst CPU (13.3), on the Hub's side.** The Actuator can't see throttling (Prometheus, or the kubelet through `nodes/proxy`, too wide a permission). The Hub's hourly read: an automated container with a CPU limit, not Guaranteed, throttled in more than 25% of periods, gets its CPU limit raised by 25% of its template's limit a step, `max` times at most, on every pod in place and on new ones by the webhook; requests untouched; not CronJobs. It expires after `expires` unless the Hub renews it, which it does while the container's CPU peak stays above 90% of its template's limit.
  - **Seen.** A step-up and a burst: an Event on the Workload, an Actuation (new outcomes) on the Automation page, where Revert works on it, and one Slack line in the operator channel saying what to change in the manifest, under 12.9c's cap; their expiry an Actuation, no Slack. A startup boost: an Event on the pod only (it happens at every start). A Triage Report links a Reflex by a line the Hub itself adds to the Report's Slack thread, nothing the model sees: no eval rerun.
  - **Slices.** 13.1a: `reflexes.oom` in the Contract, the Hub lists automated containers, the Actuator sees and attributes their OOM kills and reports them, changing nothing. 13.1b: the step-up (all pods, the webhook, the caps, the cluster's budget, the Rollback interplay). 13.1c: expiry and the Hub's renewal, the Slack line, the Triage thread's line, the Automation page and Revert. 13.4: the startup boost. 13.3a: burst CPU on the Hub (trigger, renewal). 13.3b: burst CPU in the Actuator. Live checks on kind after 13.1c and at the end.
  - Looked up for it (2026-11-12, official docs unless said): lowering a memory limit in place by version (above); VPA's OOM bump (`--oom-bump-up-ratio` 1.2, `--oom-min-bump-up-bytes` 100 MiB); kube-startup-cpu-boost raises requests and limits by a percentage and reverts on Ready or a fixed time; the `SystemOOM` Event from the kubelet's OOM watcher (no primary source found for how the two kinds read on the pod: to confirm on kind).
- Attribution's gap, looked up and resolved 2026-11-12 (the Operator's "resolve it", after 13.1a), for 13.1b to build. The facts (kubelet v1.34.0, containerd v2.0.0, cadvisor v0.52.1, the kernel's cgroup v2 document): the kubelet sets a memory limit on the `kubepods` cgroup, the node's capacity less what is reserved, whatever `enforceNodeAllocatable` says, and on a pod's cgroup when every container has a limit (their sum, plus overhead); a kill at either is a cgroup's kill, so the kubelet says no `SystemOOM`; containerd marks a container `OOMKilled` when its cgroup's `oom_kill` count rises, which the kernel counts for "any kind of OOM killer"; so nothing on a Pod or in an Event tells such a kill from one at the container's own limit, nor does `container_oom_events_total`, which cadvisor counts on the killed task's cgroup. Only the container's usage would, and Prometheus has it by samples. Resolved: the Actuator still decides alone and within a minute, and a kill with no `SystemOOM` of a container with a limit still gets the step-up. Erring the other way would leave a container at its own limit crash-looping, the case the Reflex exists for; the step-up raises the request with the limit, which is also what a container killed over its request under a limit above it needs (its `oom_score_adj` falls as its request rises); and it is bounded and said. One rule added: while a Sizing that lowered that container's memory request is in its watch hour, an OOM kill of it is a Rollback as today, never a step-up, because a lowered request raises its `oom_score_adj` and the node's overcommit, so it can bring a kill above the container's limit. Not chosen: the Hub confirming from Prometheus that the container was at its limit (a 30-second sample can miss the spike, and the step-up would come a minute or two late, past this phase's done-when). 13.1a's report says what is known: "with a memory limit of 256Mi, its node not out of memory (container-limit)".
- 13.1a built 2026-11-12: `actuator: reflexes: {oom: {}}` in the Contract (refused without a cluster that runs an Actuator; `max` and `expires` are refused until 13.1b and 13.1c give them behaviour, so the grilled example above is not valid yet). With it the Hub lists, in `/actuator/desired`'s `oom`, the cluster's containers under Automation (a Sizing ready or not; none of a Workload whose Automation ended, of a container reverted since its approval, of a kind the Actuator didn't name, or under the Kill switch); an Actuator of 0.46.0 or older ignores the field. Each minute the Hub answers, the Actuator reads each listed namespace's pods once (a pod's Workload told from its owners and labels, as the webhook does: `workload_of`) and takes the OOM kills of the last 10 minutes (`OOMKilled` as the runtime reports it, running again or ended for good, so a CronJob's run too). Attribution (13.2), from the source (kubelet v1.34.0, cadvisor v0.52.1, Linux 6.12: the kubelet emits `SystemOOM` for an OOM kill no memory cgroup's limit caused, whichever process the kernel chose, a pod's included, and for none a cgroup's limit caused), not seen live: node-level when the container has no memory limit or its node has a `SystemOOM` Event within 2 minutes of the kill (Events in `default`: a Role in `deploy/actuator/rbac.yaml`, which covers every Event there; shipped by default, the Operator's choice, and a client may remove it); else container-limit, and so too where the Events can't be read, said in the report and logged once. One Actuation `oom_killed` a container (migration 0031), its limit, its pods (five named), which kind each was; every 10 minutes at most, with its pods killed since its last report (a pod shows its last kill only, so pods are counted, not kills); 50 containers a minute at most and none while reports the Hub hasn't taken wait, so sightings can't push a Rollback's report out of the 200 kept across a restart; when it last reported is kept in memory only (after a restart the same report again, stored once; one with a newer kill is a second row). Nothing changes in the cluster, and an OOM kill in a watch hour is still a Rollback. The Hub stores it, tells no one, and the Automation page and Revert ignore it (a container's last Actuation and what it holds are as before; one only ever seen is no row): until 13.1c a sighting is visible only in the Actuator's log and the `actuations` table. Container-limit therefore also covers a kill at a limit above the container's own, which nothing it reads tells apart: see the resolution below. Not done: the pause annotation isn't read for a listed Workload nothing is held for (reporting changes nothing; 13.1b must read it before it acts); a kill the runtime reports as `Error`, 137 is not seen; no Event on the Workload. Isolation: two Contracts with the Reflex each get only their own containers, and one without it none. Mutation-checked (28 of 28 killed).
- 13.1a checked live 2026-11-12, the container-limit half only, on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, a worker, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac listing `leaky` and `calm` (no model calls, no cloud): Deployments `leaky` and `other` (2 pods each, memory limit 64Mi, filling memory 15 seconds after each start) and `calm`. With its Role in `default` (it could list Events there, not in `kube-system`, and no nodes): one report the first minute it could list the namespace's pods, `leaky` `oom_killed`, 2 pods, memory 64Mi, run 0, both container-limit; nothing of `other`, killed as often but not listed, nor of `calm`; none the minute after, the kills going on (5 restarts by then); every pod still at its 50m and 32Mi; and no `SystemOOM` Event in `default`, as the source says of a kill at a cgroup's limit. Then the RoleBinding in `default` deleted and the Actuator's pod too: a report within seconds of its restart (its memory of the last one gone; a newer kill, so a second row), both pods container-limit with the node's Events not read and the API server's refusal, logged once; no other in the two minutes after. It showed that refusal repeated for each pod, now said once at the report's end. First the Actuator's manifest had been applied before the namespace existed, so it could not list its pods: it said so each minute and went on. Not checked live: node-level (a `SystemOOM` on kind means exhausting the Docker VM the Operator's Hub runs in), and the Hub's side (unit-tested). The cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- 13.1b built 2026-11-12: ADR 0005 and the glossary's Step-up first. `reflexes.oom.max` in the Contract (2, more than 1, 4 at most), sent as `oom_max` in `/actuator/desired`; an Actuator given none (a Hub of 13.1a) only reports. A container-limit kill is answered by a Step-up (`step_up`): memory limit to the larger of 1.2 times and 100 MiB more in whole Mi, the request by the same factor (equal ones stay equal), never past `max` times the template's limit, on every pod in place and at once, one pod's refusal leaving the others theirs, a lost pod untouched. One a container every 10 minutes and 10 a cluster an hour, both kept in `state.json` with each container's step. Only a kill of a pod at the current step earns the next; one below it is brought to it; a kill from before a step every pod has is only said. Not made, and said with the kill: paused, a VPA, an HPA on memory, a `resizePolicy` that restarts for memory, no memory limit in the template, a LimitRange or a ResourceQuota (`limits.memory` too). The Sizing beside it: the step starts from the larger of the template's request and a Sizing's, never a lowered one; the hold gives up its memory and keeps its cpu; no Sizing sets that container's memory while it lasts (its Actuation says so, and one asking memory only is skipped, once); a Revert drops it. The Rollback interplay: `trouble()` leaves an OOM-killed pod to the Reflex when it looks this minute (the Hub answered with `oom_max`, the container listed, no report waiting) unless the Sizing lowered its memory request (a template with only a limit asks that much: found by a test); a node-level kill rolls back a hold that lowered it, at any time; a kill answered by a Rollback earns no step; where the Reflex doesn't look, a Rollback as before. A crash that is no OOM kill stays the Sizing's. From review, each pinned: only a kill since the pod got its requests is the Reflex's (an `OOMKilled` status from before excused a pod NotReady after a resize); a pod is at a step by the limit its status says it was given, not the one its spec asks (a resize a node can't fit stays asked on 1.33 and 1.34, and each kill would have earned a further step); a step is kept and saved before a pod is touched and undone when none took it (not counted either); none while that container's Sizing rolls out. Outcome `stepped_up` (migration 0032), an Event `MemorySteppedUp`; the Automation page and Revert ignore it until 13.1c. No isolation test of its own: the Hub's part is one number beside 13.1a's list, tested there with two Contracts. Mutation-checked with the rest of the slice, below.
- 13.1b, the rest, built the same day: new pods start at their container's Step-up, limit and request, from the webhook (`stepped_for`: where the pod asks what the template asked then, its QoS class kept, never a DaemonSet's, which is raised in place the minute after, as 12.8a has it; never under the Kill switch or paused); each minute the Hub answers, pods asking less than their container's Step-up are brought to it in place and a Step-up whose template's memory a person changed, or whose Workload is gone, is handed back and said (`keep_steps`); a CronJob's Step-up is for its next runs' pods (its parallelism counted against a quota), and an OOM kill in its watched first run is the Reflex's on the same terms; a Sizing says when its memory was left to a Step-up. From a second review, each pinned: a Step-up's end has its own outcome, `step_ended` (as `handed_back` it became the container's last Actuation on the Automation page, and its Revert button went while a Sizing's cpu was still held); a pod is only ever raised ("brought to its Step-up" would have lowered one above it, left from a Step-up dropped since); a resize a node can't fit, which 1.33 and 1.34 keep pending, is put back the minute after to what the pod was given and the pod left for good (`left`), or a later Sizing's rollout would have read it as its own Infeasible pod and evicted it: a Reflex never causes an eviction. Not built here, 13.1c's: expiry, the Slack line, the Automation page, and lowering running pods in place at a Revert, a hand-back or the Reflex switched off; until then the Reflex should not run on a real cluster. Mutation-checked on the final code: 89 mutations of the whole slice's rules, each killed; two more showed a redundant line, removed.
- 13.1b checked live 2026-11-12 on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, two workers, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac sending `oom_max` 2 (no model calls, no cloud): Deployment `leaky` (a pod a worker) and DaemonSet `agent`, each container filling memory until killed at its 128Mi limit (request 64Mi); the second worker's memory requests filled to 30Mi free. **The step**: within the minute of the kills both were stepped, limit 128Mi to 228Mi and request 64Mi to 114Mi, `2 of 2 pods in place`, an Event `MemorySteppedUp` on each. On the roomy worker the kubelet gave both pods 228Mi and 114Mi in place, with no restart but the kill's own. **No room**: on the full worker both resizes stayed `PodResizePending` (Deferred; this version refuses at once only what a node could never fit); the minute after, both pods were put back to 128Mi and 64Mi, the pending resize gone, reported `1 left as they were: their node has no room`, and never tried again though they went on being killed. **Kept**: both steps, the hour's two and the left pods were in `state.json`. **New pods**: the Deployment's pod deleted, its replacement started at 228Mi and 114Mi from the webhook; the DaemonSet's pod deleted, its replacement started from its template's 128Mi and was raised in place within the minute, `brought to its Step-up ... on 1 of 2 pods`, no restart. It showed a left pod's later kill said as "before its last Step-up": now said for what it is. Not checked live: a further step and the cap (10 minutes on), a CronJob's runs, a Guaranteed pod, a resize the API server refuses at once, the interplay with a Sizing's Rollback (all unit-tested). The cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- 13.1c built 2026-11-12. **Its term**: `reflexes.oom.expires` (7 days, 1 hour to 90 days), sent as `oom_expires`, and each listed container's memory peak over its Sizing's window (`peak`, the Sizing's own basis). `expires` after its last step or its last renewal, a Step-up is kept another term while that peak is above its template's limit or not known, and ends (`step_ended`, an Event `StepUpExpired`) when it is within it or the Hub no longer lists the container; none from the Hub (one of 13.1b): never. **Its pods**: back to the template's memory in place only where the peak says they fit (so no resize hangs above what a pod uses, as 1.34 leaves one, and none is killed), else left until they start again; a 1.33 refusal said pod by pod; a Revert does the same. **Slack**: a Step-up made and a container still OOM-killed at its cap (once a day, by the kills' own time) are kept as still to tell and go through 12.9c's teller, its cap and its retries (`to_tell`; the store takes the rule from the Hub); pods only brought to one, a kill only seen and its end are not posted; a Step-up after three Rollbacks does not say Automation ended again. **The Automation page**: a row's Step-up limit (`stepped`), a container with only one a row too, Revert offered for either and accepted by the Hub; a Step-up counts until its end or a Revert; another cluster's or Contract's is never shown (tested with two Contracts). **A Triage's thread**: one line above the Report for each Step-up made, from an hour before the Triage began, on a Workload its Alerts name (a kind's label, or a `pod` label beginning with the Workload's name and a dash, in its namespace); the Hub's own, nothing the model sees, no eval rerun. Console types regenerated. Not done: a Step-up brought back in place is tried once, not again; a line is said again if a Triage ends twice. From review, each pinned: a Step-up reported without its limit is still said, never one that stops the Rollbacks behind it from being told; a Revert lowers by the peaks of the minute before, the Hub no longer listing a reverted container in the answer that asks it; a renewal is reported (`stepped_up`, not told), so the page still knows a Step-up older than the 90 days Actuations are kept; a stored Sizing's `memory.basis` is the peak the term reads. Choices the grill left open, made here: a peak not known keeps a Step-up; a container no longer listed ends at its term without its pods being touched. Mutation-checked: 43 mutations, each killed; one more showed an untestable guard, removed.
- 13.1c checked live 2026-11-12, the Actuator's side, on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, a worker, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac sending `oom_max` 2 and a term of 150 seconds (no model calls, no cloud): Deployments `a` (2 pods), `b` and `c`, each container OOM-killed once at its 128Mi limit and calm after. All three stepped to 228Mi and 114Mi in place. **A Revert** of `b`, the Hub no longer listing it in that answer: its Step-up dropped and its pod back to 128Mi and 64Mi in place, by the peak (100Mi) of the minute before, with no restart. **The first term over**: `a`, its peak not known, kept for another term and said so; `c`, no longer listed, ended, its pod left at 228Mi. **The peak then said to be 100Mi**: at its next term `a` ended, `2 of 2 pods back to memory limit 128Mi, request 64Mi in place`, an Event `StepUpExpired`; one pod's resize showed in progress for some seconds, then both were given 128Mi, neither restarted. It showed a term of minutes said as "0 hours": now said in days, hours or minutes. Not checked live: a peak above the limit renewing (unit-tested), a 1.33 refusal, and the Hub's side (the Slack lines, the Automation page, the Triage thread's line: unit-tested, since reports made up for a check must not reach the Operator's Hub). The cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- 13.4 built 2026-11-12: the glossary's Startup boost first. `startup_boost` on a Sizing rule (1.5 to 4, refused without `automate`); the Hub sends, in `/actuator/desired`'s `boosts`, the automated containers whose rule sets it with its factor (never a CronJob's; none under the Kill switch; each Contract's Actuator its own). The webhook (`boosted`) multiplies a new pod's CPU limit and writes the container's own limit in the pod's annotation `squero-actuator/startup-boost`; not for a Guaranteed pod (one Guaranteed by its limits alone included), a container without a CPU limit, a CronJob's run, a paused Workload, under the Kill switch, or a pod that already carries the annotation. Each minute, the Hub reachable or not (`keep_boosts`, the list kept in `state.json`), a boosted container whose pod is Ready, that has run for 5 minutes or that restarted gets its own limit back in place, with an Event `StartupBoostEnded` on the pod (with the pod's uid, which `kubectl describe pod` selects Events by); no Actuation, no migration, no API the console reads, nothing the model sees (no eval rerun). **Chosen beyond the grill**, each the safer reading: the 5 minutes count from the container's own start, not the pod's (`status.startTime` is from before its image is pulled and its init containers run, which could use the boost up before the container ran at all); "no boost on a container's restart" read to the letter, a container that restarts while boosted loses the boost at once (which also bounds a crash loop that never gets Ready); no boost for a pod with pod-level resources (`spec.resources`), whose limit a container's may not pass; a Revert of a container, or Automation ended by three Rollbacks, stops its boost too (the Hub lists only what is under Automation now); no boost in a namespace whose LimitRange sets a cpu maximum or limit-to-request ratio or whose ResourceQuota counts `limits.cpu` (either could refuse the boosted pod's creation; the whole namespace, whatever room they leave, said once in the log); none for a container whose `resizePolicy` restarts it for cpu (giving the limit back would restart it); a VPA is read each minute, not at the pod's creation (the webhook has 2 seconds and reads nothing); a container with a CPU limit and no request gets that limit written as its request, so the request can't follow the boost; the annotation is read as anyone's word (only ever a limit lowered to it, never under the request); under the Kill switch or a pause a pod boosted before keeps its higher limit (the Actuator resizes nothing then); a namespace is read one minute more after its last boost was wanted and then until no pod of it still carries one, so a rule removed mid-start still gets its limit back; the limit comes back up to a minute after the pod is Ready (the Actuator's interval). 69 mutations, all caught (five survived first: a Sizing's request on a limit-only container, a namespace it can't read after its rule went, a number in the annotation, a refused resize after its rule went, the webhook server passing the boosts; each got its test).
- 13.4 checked live 2026-11-13 on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, a worker, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac sending `boosts` with a factor of 2 (no model calls, no cloud). **At creation**: pods of three Deployments (cpu 100m under a limit of 200m) started under 400m with `{"app": "200m"}` in their annotation, the container's cgroup `cpu.max` at `40000 100000`; a container with a limit and no request started with request 200m under 400m; a Guaranteed pod, a CronJob's runs (listed in `boosts` all the same), and the pods of a namespace with a LimitRange cpu maximum and of one with a ResourceQuota on `limits.cpu` kept their own limit, each namespace said once in the log. **Given back**, no container restarted for it: the pods Ready after 90 seconds at the next minute (`cpu.max` `20000 100000`, the kubelet's `ResizeCompleted` beside `StartupBoostEnded` in `kubectl describe pod`); a pod never Ready 5 minutes after its container started ("5 minutes after its start"); a container that exits after 25 seconds at its first restart ("it restarted"). **The Hub stopped**: a new pod was boosted and given back; the Actuator restarted meanwhile boosted the next from its ConfigMap. **The Kill switch**: a new pod started from its own limit, the pod boosted before kept 400m while Ready for two minutes, and got 200m back the minute the switch went off. **The rule removed** while a pod was boosted: its limit came back when it was Ready, the namespace then left the Actuator's state, and the next pod was not boosted. No report reached the Hub, no error in the Actuator's log. Not checked live: a pause, a VPA, a pod with pod-level resources, a `resizePolicy` that restarts for cpu, and whether it was the API server's defaulting or the webhook that wrote the limit-only container's request (the result is the same). Cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- 13.3a built 2026-11-13: the glossary's Burst first. `actuator: reflexes: {burst: {expires: 24h}}` in the Contract (1 hour to 7 days; refused without a cluster that runs an Actuator; `max` is refused until 13.3b gives it behaviour, so the grilled example is not valid yet). With it the hourly Sizing run also reads each container's share of CFS periods throttled over the last hour (`usage.usage(recent=True)`: the window's own expression with the range changed, two more instant queries a cluster for each policy window) and keeps, in the stored row's `burst`, that share and the container's CPU peak over the last `expires` (the highest of the samples already read). `/actuator/desired` gets `burst`, the cluster's automated containers (never a CronJob's; none without the Reflex or under the Kill switch; each Contract's Actuator its own), each with `throttled` (the share, only when over a quarter) and `peak`, and `burst_run`, the run they were read in. The Actuator reads them into `Desired` and does nothing with them: no migration, no API the console reads, nothing the model sees (no eval rerun). **Chosen beyond the grill**: the trigger is over the hour before the read, not the policy's window (over 7 or 14 days a container throttled once last week would be stepped to its cap, an hour at a time); a container that ran fewer than 600 CFS periods in that hour is not told (one throttled in the only second it ran would take a place in the cluster's 10 an hour and a Slack line); a read more than two hours old tells no Burst and no peak (the Actuator then keeps what it has, as with a Step-up's unknown peak); the peak is over the last `expires`, hence its 7 days at most, the shortest built-in window; `burst_run` so that one read is one Burst (the Actuator pulls each minute); the Actuator, not the Hub, compares the peak with 90% of the template's limit, as with a Step-up (the Hub only sees the pods' limit, which a Burst has raised). Known and said in the guide: a Workload's pods are pooled, so one hot pod among many reads low; the peak is of 5-minute rates, so it tells sustained load, and a spiky container's Burst would end and be made again. **For 13.3b**: two runs can be less than an hour apart (`squero sizing refresh` stores one on demand) and then share throttled minutes, so the Actuator must also keep to one Burst a container an hour, whatever the runs; and `Throttled`'s four required fields are a commitment once an Actuator with it is released (it drops the Hub's whole answer over an item it can't read), so 13.3b may only add optional fields. 34 mutations, all caught (one survived first: the OOM Reflex alone switching the list on).
- 13.3a checked live 2026-11-13, read-only: the working tree's hourly read (`usage.collect`, `sizing.rows`, `sizing.bursting`; no Hub, no database, no Actuator, no model calls) with a synthetic Contract that sets `reflexes.burst`, against the Prometheus of a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, kube-prometheus-stack 92.11.0, the lab's workloads and three more with a CPU limit), 6 and 15 minutes after its start. The lab's own cluster was not used: its node has been stopped for two days, and was left so. The one-hour expression returns a series per container on real cAdvisor metrics, and only for containers with a CPU limit (one without has no CFS periods, so is never told). `throttled` (wants 0.5 cores under 200m): 7176 of 8795 periods, 82%, told, its peak 0.19 cores; `spiky-capped` (a minute at 0.9 cores every 15 under 200m): 322 of 8911, 3.6%, kept and not told; `blip` (3 seconds of a full core every 5 minutes under 100m): 91 of 99 periods throttled, 92%, and not told, held back by the floor (about 33 periods a blip, some 400 an hour), its peak read as 0.001 cores, the 5-minute rate hiding the spike as the guide says; a container that only sleeps: no periods. Every row was still learning (minutes of history) and carried its numbers all the same. Seen there: the floor counts a Workload's pods together, so two replicas of `blip` would pass it (written in the guide). Not checked live: the stored row and `/actuator/desired` (unit tests only), a Workload of several pods, a read through a Relay. Cluster deleted, the port-forward stopped.
- 13.1c fixed 2026-11-13 (found building 13.3b): with `reflexes.oom` switched off the Hub sends no `oom_expires`, and the Actuator read the term from that alone, so a Step-up never ended, though the guide said it did. Its term is now kept with each Step-up (`expires` in its state, set each minute the Hub sends one), as a Burst's is; one that never heard a term still never expires. 4 mutations, all caught.
- 13.3b built 2026-11-13: the Burst in the Actuator, the last slice of Phase 13. `reflexes.burst.max` in the Contract (2, more than 1, 4 at most), sent as `burst_max` with `burst_expires`; an Actuator given no `burst_max` (a Hub of 13.3a) makes none and reports none. **The step** (`burst`): for a listed container the read says is throttled, once a read, its CPU limit up by a quarter of its template's limit, whole millicores, never past `max` times it, on every live pod in place (`limit_cpu`: only ever raised); requests untouched; kept in `state.json` before a pod is touched; a Burst every pod refused is undone and not counted. **Not made**, said as an Actuation `throttled` ("No Burst: ...", a standing reason once a day, 50 containers a minute at most): no CPU limit in the template, a paused Workload, a Guaranteed pod, a `resizePolicy` that restarts for cpu, a VPA, a Sizing still rolling out, a LimitRange's maximum or ratio, a ResourceQuota that counts `limits.cpu`, its cap, the hour's budget, no live pod, a CronJob. **New pods**: the webhook starts them at the Burst's limit while their own is still the template's then, a Startup boost on top of it and given back to the Burst's limit. **Each minute the Hub answers** (`keep_bursts`): a pod below it brought up; dropped, touching no pod, when a person changed the template's CPU limit or the Workload is gone; at its term kept while the Hub's peak is above 90% of the template's limit or unknown, else ended with every pod above the template's limit back to it in place (`burst_back`). A Revert drops it and lowers its pods. **The Hub**: migration 0033 (outcomes `burst`, `burst_ended`, `throttled`); one Slack line a Burst made, saying what to set in the manifest, and one a day for a container still throttled at its cap (its own day, apart from an OOM cap's); the Automation page shows a Burst's CPU limit (`burst`), a container with only one is a row and can be reverted; a Triage's thread names a Burst as it does a Step-up. Nothing the model sees: no eval rerun. **Chosen beyond the grill**: a container's Bursts at least 45 minutes apart, on the Actuator's clock (reads are an hour apart and a step lands up to a minute after its read, so a full hour would decline every other read; two runs minutes apart share their throttled minutes); the last read handled for each container is kept across a restart, a declined one too, and a Burst that ends is not made again from the read handled then; the term is kept with each Burst, so one still ends after the Reflex is switched off; at most 5 of the cluster's 10 an hour are Bursts and OOM kills are looked at first, so a crash loop's Step-up never waits behind throttling; no Burst in a namespace whose ResourceQuota counts `limits.cpu`, whatever room it leaves (an HPA answering the same load makes pods that each ask the Burst's limit); declined while a Sizing of that container rolls out, as a Step-up is, but tried again each minute of that read (a new Sizing and its read's throttling arrive in one answer, and the rollout takes minutes); the reads handled are forgotten by read, not by listing, so a Revert followed by an approval within one read makes no second Burst; the 50 a minute count containers looked at, not reports made; an HPA on cpu does not decline it (requests are untouched); a reason that stands is said once a day, not once a read. 120 mutations, all caught (four survived first: the minute's upkeep and the peaks through `step`, a read without a number, another cluster's Burst on the page; the 15 on the trigger's loop run again after a review changed it).
- 13.3b checked live 2026-11-13, the Actuator's side, on a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, a worker, the Actuator on the control plane), the working tree's image, a stand-in Hub on the Mac sending `burst` with `burst_max` 2 and a term of 3 minutes (no model calls, no cloud, no Prometheus: the shares and peaks were the stand-in's). **The step**: `hot` (two pods, cpu 100m under 200m) and `warm` went to 250m in place the first minute, no container restarted, the container's cgroup `cpu.max` from `20000` to `25000`, an Event `CpuBurst` on each Deployment; a Guaranteed pod, a container without a CPU limit and one in a namespace whose ResourceQuota counts `limits.cpu` each got a `throttled` report with its reason, said once. **New pods**: a replaced pod of `hot` started at 250m; `warm`, which also has a Startup boost of 2, started at 500m with `{"app": "250m"}` in its annotation and was given back to 250m when Ready. **A second read** a minute later: "No Burst: burst 1 minutes ago: one a container every 45 minutes" for both, the standing reasons not said again. **The term**: after 3 minutes `hot` (peak 50m) ended, both pods back to 200m in place, `cpu.max` `20000`, an Event `BurstExpired`; `warm` (peak 190m, above 90% of 200m) was kept for another term. **A Revert** of `warm` dropped its Burst and put its pod back to 200m. **A new read** after `hot`'s end made a new Burst; then its template's limit set by hand to the 250m the report named: the Burst dropped ("its template's CPU limit changed: 200m to 250m"), an Event `HandedBack`, the rollout's pods at their template's 250m, and no new Burst from that read. The ConfigMap held the Bursts and the reads handled; no error in the Actuator's log. Not checked live: the Hub's side of it (migration 0033, the Slack lines, the Automation page, the Triage thread's line: unit tests only), the Kill switch, a pause, a LimitRange, the hour's budget, and the whole path from real throttling in Prometheus to a Burst (13.3a's read and this were each checked on their own). Cluster deleted, the stand-in Hub stopped.
- *Done when:* an OOM-killed pod's next replica gets more memory within a minute, and the step-up expires on schedule.

### Phase 14: Horizontal (6 slices, grilled 2026-11-12)

- 14.1 HPA and KEDA minimum replicas from percentiles. 14.2 Weekly profiles with lead time. 14.3 Re-anchoring the HPA target after a Sizing changes requests. 14.4 Backtests with an error budget.
- Grilled 2026-11-12 (three rounds, all recommended):
  - **Read-only.** The Hub recommends and the client's GitOps applies: nothing in this phase writes to an HPA or a ScaledObject, and no permission is added (Argo CD with self-heal and Flux both put a patched HPA back unless told to ignore the field). Only Workloads that have an HPA or a KEDA ScaledObject; one with a fixed replica count gets nothing yet. Everything is read from Prometheus (kube-state-metrics' HPA series), as Sizings are; KEDA through the HPA it creates (`keda-hpa-<name>`), since KEDA has no metric for a ScaledObject's minimum, maximum or target. Writing a minimum is Deferred, with its own grill and an ADR (a new Actuator write).
  - **Need.** The HPA's own "desired replicas" never reads below its minimum, so the Hub computes what a Workload needed: current replicas x current value / target value, for any metric the HPA scales on (resource, per container, pods, object, external), the largest when it has several. "Unknown" where the series are missing, for a Workload with two HPAs, or a per-container metric on a kube-state-metrics older than v2.19.0 (no `container` label). The Hub stores each Workload's peak need per hour itself (one table, kept 8 weeks, filled at first from what Prometheus still has), because a weekly profile needs more weeks than most Prometheus keep.
  - **Floor (14.1).** A low percentile of the hourly need over the Workload's Sizing policy's window: the 10th under `production`, the 25th under `high-availability`, a policy setting; none under `off`. Never below 2 unless today's minimum already is; a minimum of 0 (KEDA's scale to zero) is left alone. Shown against today's minimum: a saving when lower, a risk when higher. Its saving is its own line, the replica-hours removed priced at the Sizing's requests, so it adds to Waste without counting a replica twice; available only, and realized once kube-state-metrics shows the HPA's minimum lowered. One risk line when the need was above the HPA's maximum ("needed more than its maximum in 6% of hours"); no maximum is recommended.
  - **Requests under an HPA (14.3).** A Sizing of a request an HPA divides by (`Utilization`, cpu or memory) is marked "held by its HPA" (today the Hub shows it as available and the Actuator skips it), with the one-time change that frees it: the target as `AverageValue`, the same threshold as an absolute number (the target percentage times the pod's summed requests, all containers; one container's for a per-container metric). After it requests play no part in the HPA and the Actuator resizes freely: it only ever skipped `Utilization` targets. For KEDA's HPA, the same change on the ScaledObject's trigger (`metricType: AverageValue`); a wrong guess of whose HPA it is only shows the wrong snippet. Its Waste still counts: one manifest line recovers it. Phase 13's rule stays: no OOM step-up for a container whose memory an HPA scales on. The Actuator never patches a target percentage.
  - **Weekly profile (14.2).** For each hour of the week, the median of that hour's need across the stored weeks (one odd week changes nothing), never below the Floor; shown as blocks of hours with one Floor each, in `sizing.timezone` (new, UTC by default), each block starting `sizing.lead_time` early (new, 15 minutes by default; measuring it per Workload from its pods' time to Ready can come later). For a KEDA Workload, a ready `cron` trigger per block to paste into its ScaledObject (KEDA takes the trigger asking for most replicas); a plain HPA has no schedule of its own and gets the profile with a note saying so. No generated CronJobs that patch `minReplicas`.
  - **Backtest (14.4).** The newest full week replayed hour by hour against a profile built from the weeks before it (all of those kept, three to seven: changed 2026-11-14 from "the three before it"), so a backtest needs 4 stored weeks. Error: the replica-hours it was off by, too few plus too many, as a share of the replica-hours needed; both parts shown, and the cost of the too many. Budget: 20%, fixed (a setting once real backtests show what is normal). Over it the Workload shows "load isn't weekly enough" and keeps only its Floor. A gap under 10% is nothing the HPA acts on (its tolerance, both ways).
  - **Seen.** The console's Sizing views (the Floor beside each autoscaled Workload, its profile, backtest and the manifest change to copy) and one line in the weekly digest. The Triage agent's sizing tool is unchanged: no eval rerun.
  - **Terms.** *Floor*: the minimum replicas the Hub computes for a Workload with an autoscaler. *Weekly profile*: its Floors by hour of the week. A Sizing stays per replica.
  - **Slices.** 14.1a: HPAs read from kube-state-metrics, each Workload's hourly need stored; a load generator with a weekly pattern started in the lab, for 14.4's live check four weeks on. 14.3: Sizings held by an HPA, with the `AverageValue` change (early: it frees savings on autoscaled Workloads at once); live check on kind (an HPA switched, the request cut, the replica count unchanged). 14.1b: the Floor, its saving and risks, the console and the digest line. 14.1c: the Floor's saving realized once kube-state-metrics shows the HPA's minimum lowered. 14.2: the Weekly profile, `sizing.timezone`, `sizing.lead_time`, KEDA's `cron` triggers. 14.4: the backtest and its budget, tested on a synthetic Monday peak. Their minimums of history are covered time, by 9.7's helper.
  - Looked up for it (2026-11-12; Kubernetes v1.34 and kube-state-metrics source, KEDA, Argo CD and Flux docs): utilization is the pods' total usage over their total requests, a pod's request the sum over its containers (sidecars included; one container without a request fails it); `AverageValue` reads no requests and compares the pods' mean usage; a target above 100% is valid; the tolerance is 0.1 both ways; `kube_horizontalpodautoscaler_spec_min_replicas`, `_spec_max_replicas`, `_status_current_replicas`, `_status_desired_replicas`, `_status_target_metric` (stable) and `_info`, `_spec_target_metric` (experimental; to confirm on the lab), the last two for all five metric kinds with `metric_name`, `metric_target_type` (`value`, `utilization`, `average`) and `container`. Not found in a primary source: whether KEDA puts back a direct edit of its HPA.
- 14.1a built 2026-11-13: `usage.needs` reads each cluster's HPAs from kube-state-metrics (`_info`, `_status_current_replicas`, `_spec_target_metric`, `_status_target_metric`, in the Contract's namespaces and labels like every read; through a Relay as it is, the Hub's own PromQL) and the hourly run stores each Workload's Need as every UTC hour's peak in table `needs` (migration 0034), kept 8 weeks; nothing is shown and no API changed. The lab gets `weekly`, a Deployment under an HPA (CPU 50%, 2 to 5) whose load follows the wall clock and is split between its pods (counted from a headless Service), needing 1, 3 and 7 replicas over the week, and metrics-server in `make lab`. Changed from the grilling, each put to the Operator and confirmed 2026-11-13: with no replicas the Need is 0, not unknown (nothing ran and nothing was asked; it gives a scale-to-zero Workload's profile its idle hours); the replicas counted are the smaller of a step's and the step before's (never across a gap in the series), because Kubernetes' HPA reports a scale-up's new replica count at once with the utilization of the pods that already have metrics (`replica_calculator.go`: unready and missing pods' metrics are dropped before the utilization it reports), which read as 3 replicas at 83% going to 5 needing 8, in the same hours every week, where a profile's median would keep it; it doesn't help while new pods take longer than a step to report; and a per-container metric on a kube-state-metrics before 2.19 is not unknown, because the series can't show it is one and its current value still pairs with its own target. The limit that leaves: there, an HPA with two per-container metrics of one resource reads as one metric. Chosen beyond the grill: the rules hold per step (two HPAs only while both exist; an HPA moved to another Workload counts for each in its time; one of several targets without a current value makes the step unknown, never a guess from the rest; a target of zero is unknown); the first read goes 8 weeks back at a 9-minute step, each later one from the newest hour stored at a minute's, so a Hub that was down catches up; an hour read twice keeps the larger; a failed read of Needs is logged and never fails the Sizing run; a cluster without an HPA costs one query an hour, one whose HPAs never give a Need is read 8 weeks back each hour (marked in the code). Samples are instants, so a peak between two is missed. Isolation tested with both demo Contracts on one Prometheus; 45 mutations, each failing a test.
- 14.1a checked live 2026-11-13 19:15 to 19:21 UTC on the lab (kube-state-metrics 2.20.0, Kubernetes 1.37): metrics-server 0.9.0 (chart 3.14.0), the new load script and `weekly` with its Service and HPA applied, and left there. The HPA held 2 pods at 25% to 26% of their request against 50% (each pod burning 26m of the 50m shared load, so the pods count their peers), and the working tree's read gave a Need of 1.00 to 1.04 at every step, below the HPA's minimum of 2, where the HPA's own desired replicas read 2. All four series are there, `_info` and `_spec_target_metric` included, with the labels the read uses; a resource metric's current value comes as `utilization` and as `average`, and the read took the one of the target's type; the minute before the HPA had a utilization gave no Need. One stored run (`sizing.store_run`, a throwaway database on the test Postgres, dropped after; no Hub, no model calls) applied migration 0034 and stored the one row, its next read starting at that hour. Not checked yet, the load being at its base until Monday 08:00 UTC: a Need between the minimum and the maximum, one above the maximum (Mondays 09:00 to 12:00), and what the hour of a scale-up reads. The Mac Hub (0.47.0) reads no Needs before the next release.
- 14.3 built 2026-11-13: `usage.scaled` reads each cluster's HPA `Utilization` targets on cpu or memory from kube-state-metrics as they are at the run (`_info`, `_spec_target_metric`; plain selectors, so a switched target is gone at the next run), and `sizing.rows` marks a ready Sizing that would change a request such an HPA divides by as `held`: the HPA, its percentage, the same threshold as an absolute value and the manifest change as text, a plain HPA's `target` or, for `keda-hpa-<name>`, the ScaledObject's trigger (form taken from KEDA's 2.21 docs: `metricType` beside `type`, `metadata.value` a quoted quantity, `metadata.containerName`). Shown by `sizing show`, the console's container detail and the Hub MCP's `sizings`; stored in the run's rows; Waste, risks and the Actuator untouched (it already skipped only `Utilization` targets). Chosen beyond the grill: only a Sizing that changes the request is held (a kept one has nothing to free); a per-container metric holds its container alone, a pod's every container; the sum takes every container's request, learning and excluded ones too; a `container` label that names no container of the Workload is the scrape target's own (kube-state-metrics scraped without `honor_labels`, or before 2.19), so the metric reads as the whole pod's, the grill's known wrong form; the comment above the change names the requests the value came from and says to apply it before any request changes; with no request to go by it is held with no value; two HPAs on a Workload show the first by name; a failed read marks nothing. The Hub MCP's `sizing` (one Workload) shares the Triage agent's renderer, so it is unchanged and no eval reruns: a test holds that the agent's tool gives the same for a held row. No digest line: the phase's one comes with 14.1b. Isolation tested with both demo Contracts on one Prometheus; 32 mutations, each failing a test.
- 14.3 checked live 2026-11-13 (no Hub, no database, no model calls), in three parts. On the lab, read-only, with `weekly` on a one-hour-history policy: its CPU Sizing (100m to 30m) read as held by its HPA at 50%, the change shown `averageValue: 50m` (50% of the pod's 100m), its Waste counted. On the lab, that change applied to the HPA as the Hub printed it: the next read, 90 seconds later, held nothing (the `utilization` series gone from Prometheus at once), the Need still read 1.04 against the `AverageValue` target, and the replica count stayed 2; the HPA put back, the request read as held again. On a throwaway kind cluster (Kubernetes 1.37, metrics-server, `squero:ci` built from the working tree, a stand-in Hub; deleted after), `weekly` with three times the load so its HPA held 3 pods at 50m each against 50% of 100m: an Approved Sizing of 60m was skipped (`cpu left to its HPA, which scales on its utilization`); the same printed change applied to that HPA, a newer run was applied, 3 of 3 pods resized in place to 60m (the canary's 10 minutes, then one a minute, no restart); and the HPA read 3 replicas, 3 desired and 49m to 52m a pod in every one of 46 samples 20 seconds apart, from the first resize to 6 minutes after the last, with no rescale event. Not checked live: KEDA's form on a ScaledObject, a per-container metric, and the console page against a real Hub.
- 14.1b grilled 2026-11-13 (all recommended): the percentile of the Needs is rounded up, as the HPA rounds (1.04 is 2); with containers under different policies the highest percentile decides, and a Workload all `off` gets no Floor; the setting is `replicas: {percentile}` beside `cpu` and `memory`, 0 to 0.5, 0.10 under production and batch and 0.25 under high-availability; a first Floor needs the policy's `history` of stored Needs, with the hours it goes by shown; the saving is, per stored hour, the minimum less the larger of the Floor and that hour's Need rounded up, when positive, averaged, times the pod's requests at their Sizings (a container without one at its request), shown per Workload and as one Contract total in the console and the digest, not in the Cost page's groups yet; realizing it once the minimum is lowered is its own slice, 14.1c.
- 14.1b built 2026-11-13: `Policy.replicas.percentile`; `usage.bounds` reads each HPA's minimum and maximum from kube-state-metrics as they are at the run; `sizing.floors` computes each autoscaled Workload's Floor from the stored Needs, with its saving and its two risks (`minimum_below_floor`, `needed_above_maximum`), stored per cluster in table `floors` (migration 0035), each run replacing the last (an HPA gone takes its Floor with it; a failed read keeps the last); shown by `sizing show` (which reads the Needs Prometheus still has, having no database), the console's Workloads page (Autoscaled Workloads, with the Contract's total), the Hub MCP's `sizings` (`floors`, masked and filtered like the rest) and one digest line, said only when a minimum can come down or is below its Floor. The Triage agent's tool reads the stored rows, not this, so no eval reruns. Changed from the grilling, each put to the Operator and confirmed 2026-11-13: KEDA gives the HPA of a ScaledObject with `minReplicaCount: 0` a minimum of 1 (`GetHPAMinReplicas`, `scaledobject_types.go`) and scales to zero itself, so "a minimum of 0 is left alone" never shows for KEDA; a Workload whose HPA's replicas were 0 at some time in the Contract's longest policy window (one more read each run, `min_over_time` of `_status_current_replicas`) is taken to scale to zero and left alone as well. A Need of 0 is no such sign: an idle Workload held at its minimum stores one too. And 9.7's "the same helper" does not fit a Need: a missing hour is unknown, not idle (no replicas is stored as 0), so only the Workload's own stored hours count, each an hour, and never the hour under way, whose peak is partial; a gap still counts for nothing. Chosen beyond the grill: the window and history are those of the policy whose percentile decides; Needs being kept 8 weeks, a Floor looks back 8 weeks at most and asks for 4 weeks of history at most; two HPAs or an unknown minimum give no Floor, with the reason; a Floor equal to the minimum says nothing; the risks are the Floor's own, not added to the Workload's. Isolation tested with both demo Contracts on one Prometheus; 68 mutations, each failing a test (merging `replicas` into its base's is an equivalent one while the setting has one field).
- 14.1b checked live 2026-11-14 00:55 to 00:58 UTC on the lab (no Hub, no model calls), `weekly` on a one-hour-history policy. Read-only: its HPA's minimum 2 and maximum 5 read from kube-state-metrics, its replicas never at 0; from the 5 complete hours of Needs Prometheus had (1.04 each, the hour under way left out) its Floor was 2, equal to its minimum, so no saving, no risk and no digest line. One stored run (`sizing.store_run`, a throwaway database on the test Postgres, dropped after) applied migration 0035 and gave the same Floor back with its run's time. The HPA's minimum raised to 3 for one read (the HPA went to 3 pods): Floor 2 against a minimum of 3, `1 replica fewer on average: 0.03 cores, 0.06 GiB`, the pod at its Sizing of 30m, not its 100m request, and the digest's line `Floors: 1 can lower their HPA minimum (1.0 replicas fewer on average, 0.03 cores, 0.06 GiB).`; the minimum put back to 2, the saving gone at the next read. Not checked live: the two risk lines (above its maximum waits for Monday 09:00 UTC), a priced Contract, a Workload that scales to zero, and the console page against a real Hub.
- 14.1c grilled 2026-11-13 (all recommended): a drop is an HPA's minimum read lower than at the previous run while that run's Floor was below the old minimum, as a request drop needs a Sizing it followed; it counts down to that Floor and no further; it is worth, per stored hour since the drop, the old minimum less the larger of what it counts down to and that hour's Need rounded up, when positive, averaged over the last 7 days, times the pod's requests as they are now (the available saving is at the Sizings, so it adds to Waste; this is what a removed replica stops reserving), at today's prices as a monthly rate, accrued as that rate times the hours since; a minimum raised again takes it back as a rise does a request drop, and an HPA or Workload gone or a second HPA ends it, a failed read nothing; it counts inside the Realized Savings totals (Cost page, `sizing savings`, the Hub MCP's `savings`, the digest's *Realized this week*), listed apart from the request drops; no backfill.
- 14.1c built 2026-11-13, as grilled: `sizing.realize_floors` runs in the hourly run against the Floors the last run stored, before they are replaced, and keeps the drops in table `floor_drops` (migration 0036); `sizing.savings` adds each one's worth to the totals and lists them as `floors`; `sizing savings`, the Cost page's realized line and the API show them. Chosen beyond the grill: a rise takes the newest drop first and never lowers what a drop counted (a minimum from 2 back to its Floor of 3 changes nothing); the hour a drop was seen in counts once it is complete, the hour under way never; a drop is worth nothing until an hour since it is stored; an unknown minimum ends nothing; a Workload whose rows are gone is not shown. Isolation tested (one Contract's drops are never read, raised or ended by another's run); 31 mutations, each failing a test.
- 14.1c checked live 2026-11-14 02:35 to 03:01 UTC on the lab (no Hub, no model calls): three stored runs of the working tree into one throwaway database on the test Postgres (migration 0036; dropped after), `weekly` on a one-hour-history policy. With the HPA's minimum raised to 3 the first run stored a Floor of 2 under it (available: 1 replica, 0.03 cores at its Sizing). The minimum put back to 2, the second run stored one drop: from 3, counted down to 2, Floor 2, worth nothing yet. After the hour it was seen in was complete, the third run read it as 1 replica fewer on average, 0.10 cores and 0.06 GiB, the pod at its real requests (100m, 64Mi), and the digest's line `Realized this week: 0.10 cores, 0.06 GiB`. The lab ended as it was (minimum 2, 2 replicas). Not checked live: a rise taking a drop back, a drop stopping at a Floor above the new minimum, a priced Contract, and the Cost page against a real Hub.
- 14.2 grilled 2026-11-14 (all recommended): a first profile needs 3 weeks of the Workload's own stored Needs, the fewest where a median ignores one odd week (the backtest adds its verdict at 4); consecutive hours asking the same are one block, the hours at the Floor the baseline and not listed, a block starting `lead_time` early when it asks more than the hour before it and ending on the hour, no smoothing; a week equal to its Floor in every hour says "no weekly pattern"; shown in the console under the Floor, in `sizing show` and in the Hub MCP's `sizings`, no digest line; `sizing.timezone` an IANA name per Contract (UTC), `sizing.lead_time` from 0 to 2 hours (15 minutes).
- 14.2 built 2026-11-14, as grilled: `sizing.profile` computes it from the Needs kept (all 8 weeks, not the policy's window) and `sizing.floors` stores it on the Floor; `sizing.scheduled` gives KEDA's HPA a `cron` trigger per block (form from KEDA's 2.21 docs: `timezone`, `start`, `end` as 5-field cron expressions that must differ, `desiredReplicas` quoted) and a plain HPA a note. Chosen beyond the grill: one trigger per block with a single weekday, Sunday as 0, because the docs don't say the weekday field takes lists or ranges; the median of an even count of weeks is the middle two's mean, rounded up; an hour of the week stored in fewer than 3 weeks is at the Floor (the three weeks are counted in hours, so with gaps an hour could rest on one week, which would break "one odd week changes nothing"; put to the Operator and confirmed 2026-11-14); a week whose every hour asks the same above its Floor is flat too (one block the whole week would need a trigger whose start equals its end, which KEDA refuses); the lead time is whole minutes, cron having no seconds; in a zone on the half hour the blocks start and end on it; blocks are listed by when they start in the week. No eval reruns: the Triage agent's tool doesn't read Floors. 40 mutations, each failing a test.
- 14.2 checked live 2026-11-14 03:42 to 04:19 UTC, with synthetic Needs (the lab has a day of real ones), no Hub, no model calls. Hub side: four synthetic weeks of `weekly`'s own load pattern in a throwaway database on the test Postgres (dropped after), then one stored run of the working tree against the lab's Prometheus, read-only: with its real HPA (minimum 2, maximum 5) the Floor was 2, with `needed more than its maximum of 5 in 2% of hours`, and the profile the seven blocks expected (Monday 08:45 to 12:00 at 7, weekdays 07:45 to 18:00 at 3), with the note a plain HPA gets. KEDA side, a throwaway kind cluster with KEDA 2.21.0 (deleted after): a ScaledObject carrying all 10 triggers of a generated profile as the Hub printed them (UTC, a lead of 45 minutes, a Sunday block as day 0) was accepted and Ready, its HPA made. Applied inside a block of 4, the Deployment held 4 replicas; 23 seconds after that block's end at 04:00 it was back at its minimum of 2 (a 30-second scale-down window set for the check); at 04:15:10, the early start of a block whose hour begins at 05:00, it went to 6. Not checked live: a profile from real stored weeks (the lab has them from 4 December if it runs without gaps), a zone other than UTC on KEDA, and the console page against a real Hub.
- 14.4 grilled 2026-11-14 (all recommended): in an hour the Workload needed the larger of its Floor and that hour's Need rounded up (what its HPA would run with the Floor as its minimum), so the profile is judged only on what it adds above the Floor; the profile under test is built from all the stored weeks before the newest one (three to seven); over the budget the blocks and triggers are hidden and one line shows ("load isn't weekly enough: off by 34% last week (too few 21%, too many 13%); it keeps its Floor"), under it the same figures show beside the profile with the monthly cost of the too many, priced at the Sizings like the Floor's saving; before four weeks the three-week profile keeps showing, marked "not backtested yet (needs four weeks)". Grilled before (2026-11-12): the error and its two parts, the 20% budget, the 10% tolerance.
- 14.4 built 2026-11-14, as grilled; not checked live yet: `sizing.backtest` replays the newest 168 complete hours against `sizing._slots` (the profile's own hours of the week, now shared) of the Needs kept before them, and `sizing.floors` stores the verdict on the Floor's profile (`backtest`: `waiting`, `within` or `over`, with `error`, `few`, `many`, the hours replayed and the too many at the Sizings). Over the budget the profile is stored as `irregular` with no blocks, and `sizing.scheduled` is never called, so no view has triggers to show: the rule is at the source, not in each renderer. `floors_report` prices the too many with a priced Contract, outside `floors_total`. Shown by `sizing show`, the API (`Backtest` on `Profile`), the console and the Hub MCP's `sizings`; the Triage agent's `sizing` tool is unchanged, so no eval rerun, and no migration (Floors are stored as JSON; a Floor stored by 14.2 shows without a backtest until the next hourly run replaces it). Chosen beyond the grill: the newest week is the 168 complete hours before the run, not a calendar week; a verdict needs 504 stored hours before that week and 84 in it (half), and an hour never stored is skipped, never replayed as idle; the 10% tolerance is per hour, against what that hour needed, in whole numbers, so it only applies from 11 replicas up and the lab (2 to 5) never exercises it; the lead time plays no part in an hour-by-hour replay; an error of exactly 20% is within the budget; the Floor on both sides is today's, which has seen the newest week (the Floor is not what is under test); a profile that is flat or still learning has no backtest; the too many's cost is stored over the budget as well but shown only within it. Mutation check: 52 mutations, 51 killed after four tests were sharpened (the tolerance against what was held instead of needed, the too many averaged over a full week whatever was replayed, the Contract's zone not passed on, the MCP's line without the currency); one equivalent mutant (the tolerance as `0.1 * needed` in floats: equal for every multiple of ten). Limits: one replayed week, so one unusual newest week fails a good profile until the next; the verdict says how the weeks before predicted the newest, not how the shown profile will do. Live check: once the lab has four stored weeks without gaps (12 December). The lab's `weekly` repeats exactly and has a plain HPA, so that check can only show a profile within its budget with a note; the line over the budget and the hidden KEDA triggers need a week broken on purpose (the load generator's pattern changed for the newest week) or stay covered by tests only.
- The lab on a host that stays up (decided 2026-11-14: the Mac's sleeps leave gaps in its history, and 14.4 needs four weeks without them).
